Winter.of.Discontext — 11/30/2023 11:20 AM
The makrut lime (which we used to call the k*ffir lime because white people) …
Turned out to be a bad choice to plant in the nutrient-poor soil of Curaçao 🇨🇼
And resulted in the laranha [cf Sanskr. narang>Span. naranja > Archaic Engl. “a norange” > Mod Engl. “an orange” {happens all the time, see also “a nasp”, “a napron”, “a nawl”] …or the curaçao bitter orange. 
Winter.of.Discontext — 11/30/2023 11:29 AM
Which was by all accounts a quite inedible and commercially useless citrus
Until the Flemish (who traded with their neighbors, the Dutch colonial masters of Curaçao), for the dried peels, which they used to develop the liqueur that bears the island nation’s name 
Debauch — 11/30/2023 12:04 PM
I hope you mean actual bears.  But joking aside, this is interesting.
Get some sleep please.
Winter.of.Discontext — 11/30/2023 12:04 PM
Ah ah ah…
That’s the beginning of the story. See, liqueurs based on the dried peels of bitter and sweet oranges and sometimes other ingredients have a story all their own… the early popularity of curaçao in its 17th century heyday beget some offspring. Now, the standard curaçao generally was available as the sweeter orange curaçao (a staple of pre-prohibition cocktails, and now revived for the modern mixologist), and the slightly less sweet dry curaçao. The former of which has been handed down to us in the form of Gran Marnier, whilst the latter experienced a bit if a heyday, with brands vying to be the most dry, eventually leading to a style billed as “triple dry” (which I’m guessing you can translate into French easily enough). Other examples of the dry variety include Cointreau. But the sweet variety had a different history. Enter one Lucas Bols. …an entrepreneur and espiriteur who fancied himself a bit of an alchemist. Always the creative marketer, Bols added green, red, and blue dyes to his curaçao, selling the blue as Crème de Ciel ("cream of the sky" — [ed. note: good thing Mike Myers wasn’t around]). Anyway, in spite of many variants using poisonous copper compounds in the coloring, the blue curaçao was the only one with staying power, probably solely due to the fact that for generations, it was the only blue potable in the barkeep’s palette. The Bols company of Amsterdam still produces Curaçao in the tiny southern Caribbean nation, and has begun to re-issue the sweet vaguely citrusy liqueur in its full rainbow of colors, and one day I’ll get my hands on a bottle of the genuine blue curaçao. But until then, that is the why the objectively stupidest bottle in the bar is also among the most interesting. 

Did I mention I’m a fantastic bartender? No stunt pours necessary.

Winter.of.Discontext — 10/25/2023 3:03 AM
But seriously, I feel most punctuation should be left out of most works for publication, at least for fiction anyway. Add it here and there, use it in any way that makes sense to you, and only when it’s necessary for clarity or effect (which could mean all sorts of things from purely stylistic like using archaic punctuation conventions in an historical piece, or ending sentences in / to evoke poetic meter, or strategic placement of commas, hyphens, em- and en-dashes, or whatever to control the pace of a sentence, or just idiosyncratic choices to communicate a quirky tone or voice). For nonfiction, the more conventional (or at least systematic) the punctuation is, the better. Ultimately, the rules are useful only insofar as they allow the author to communicate (or obfuscate) in the manner they choose.

Winter.of.Discontext — 10/26/2023 11:49 PM
Yeah, on my brief perusal I thought his stuff looked like something I’d like to dig into more. 

As for aspect ratio, theoretically, shouldn’t make any difference in the quality or nature of the output, it’s just whether you want the canvas to be square rectangular or long and thin or whatever… The only cases where I’ve seen it make much of a difference in how the image looks is if say, you make an image that’s long and thin, and you put it on a canvas that’s wide and short, then you’re likely to get either cropping or distortion
And it’s a ratio, so of course, —ar 12:24 is exactly the same as —ar 500:1000
Are the only reason I sometimes put fairly big numbers in the aspect ratio is because I’m literally typing it randomly or semi randomly (or generating it randomly in a spreadsheet), and most of the time I want wonky non-standard image sizes. Somewhere, I have a spreadsheet that lets me choose between common aspect ratios like 8.5x11, cinemascope, 4x6, etc
Winter.of.Discontext — 10/26/2023 11:57 PM

Winter.of.Discontext — 10/27/2023 10:19 AM
Cool. I’ll keep an eye out for them. 

Most of the time, when I make really crazy aspect ratios, it’s by accident because I typed a number that was more random than I meant. I’ve got my spreadsheets set up so that they don’t make super working on this very often. but they do once in a while, and even still, I often adjust them when that happens. I do think it helps the bot a little bit if you are I’m trying to get a full length or three-quarter length portrait, to make the latter a tad taller and skinnier than you would if you were doing a face portrait.  So, I typically use 9:16 for faces, and 9:17 or 18 or sometimes 9:21 for figures

As for what you should do, do whatever you want. I do think having a couple of different go-tos is a good idea and just so you can aim for different books, like portraits are often somewhere in the range of 9:16 (though if you’re old enough to remember family photos at the mall, you’ll be familiar with 8x10s, 4x6’s, etc) . Landscapes are typically going to be the inverse of those. If you’re going for cinematic look, obviously you want it wider than it is high, but there are very specific aspect ratios for movies and television of course. “Flat”, widescreen, and Cinemascope are the most common, I think. But I usually just use 16:9 for cinematic stuff and modern tv, and 4:3 for retro TV. 

And I think that about exhausts my knowledge on the subject. 
Image
There’s a summary of cinematic aspect ratios. Not sure if the current version can do decimal aspect ratios, but you can just change things like 2.75:1  to 275:100, etc

Winter.of.Discontext — 10/20/2023 12:34 AM
I can share it with you if you like. Basically, I'm just loading it with tons of vocab now. Due to the complexity of the English language, it's hard to get entirely grammatically correct prompts, so they take a little editing or just accepting the nonsense.
Still have to make a module that adds artistic notes and one to generate random settings, but it's making some fun nonsense at the moment.
Debauch — 10/20/2023 12:39 AM
Is it similar to a semi-randomized generator that pairs words and/or phrases that you then adjust manually for grammar and whatnot?
Or is it more like a nuclear reactor that also makes coffee that sometimes needs sweetener or extra foam?
Winter.of.Discontext — 10/20/2023 12:45 AM
Basically, I set up a generalized sentence structure with subjects, objects, verbs, adjectives, subordinate clauses, etc. Then made a function that would randomize each of those based on lists of words and phrases. The extra foam is a module I just pulled in that generates novel words to include in the adjective list. The ungrammatical aspects come mostly from terms getting into the wrong lists. I'm too lazy to go through and edit lists of hundreds or thousands of words to make sure they all fit right lol
Debauch — 10/20/2023 12:46 AM

Winter.of.Discontext — 10/20/2023 12:47 AM

Winter.of.Discontext — 10/25/2023 3:03 AM
But seriously, I feel most punctuation should be left out of most works for publication, at least for fiction anyway. Add it here and there, use it in any way that makes sense to you, and only when it’s necessary for clarity or effect (which could mean all sorts of things from purely stylistic like using archaic punctuation conventions in an historical piece, or ending sentences in / to evoke poetic meter, or strategic placement of commas, hyphens, em- and en-dashes, or whatever to control the pace of a sentence, or just idiosyncratic choices to communicate a quirky tone or voice). For nonfiction, the more conventional (or at least systematic) the punctuation is, the better. Ultimately, the rules are useful only insofar as they allow the author to communicate (or obfuscate) in the manner they choose.

Debauch — 12/5/2023 2:10 PM
Interesting.
Here’s a potentially unpopular theory related to art that occurred to me after reading your last text. I’m interested in your thoughts although, given the context, after reading it you might elect not to provide feedback. Haha
There are only rare instances where I allow myself to be inspired or swayed by outside stimuli - tv, literature, social media, other people’s opinions, art, music et cetera. I limit my exposure and tell myself I don’t external stuff penetrating my brain because it influences my art to an extent that I can (or should?) no longer call it my own art but rather an extension of someone else’s inspiration and the voices of those who came before them. I like to believe that if I think of something that I don’t recall having seen or heard before (and would pass a lie detector test because I’m certain it’s a new concept for me), then that in itself makes me original.  I can use whatever it is and call it “my art” because I’m not (at least consciously) leveraging anyone else. But is it even possible to be unique in today’s world? Or do we all simply find ways of reimagining someone else’s idea or experience whether consciously or subconsciously, put our own stank on it, and package it up as “our” art because there aren’t other options?

Yes!  That’s what I mean. You might be right that it was Bradbury. I’m a fan of that, but then also I’m really not.  If we don’t listen and learn, then how as artists can we hope to grow?
🤯
Debauch — 12/5/2023 2:26 PM
Not trying to put you on the spot to solve this global issue in 5 seconds or less… haha
Winter.of.Discontext — 12/5/2023 2:27 PM
I don’t worry about it too much. We will listen and grow in one way or another even in a total vacuum. Human brains build culture whether we try to or not. Kids invent new languages. We see pictures in clouds. It’s just the way we’re wired. It may be that trying to limit outside voices and ideas will allow our own to take shape in new ways… or that it will keep us treading the same worn path others have tread because we don’t know we’re doing something that’s been done. Generally, I think creativity and originality is more in the presentation of ideas and re-connection in new ways than in coming up with truly brand new things. So, in my own work, I try to follow my ideas a good way down the rabbit hole and make them cross paths with a fair number of other ideas going down their own rabbit holes before I find my starting place in a story
Debauch — 12/5/2023 2:28 PM
Are you referring specifically to writing?
Winter.of.Discontext — 12/5/2023 2:29 PM
Well, that’s where I feel like ideas take center stage. In visual art, for me, it’s more about playing with the medium and discovering what it can do

Debauch — 12/5/2023 2:40 PM
Whatever I should call drawing/painting/digital art… dunno.  But for example with acting, and maybe your gf might have different thoughts, the goal really is to be original. Yes you are bound to some extent by the script and blocking and time pressures, but to work within these constraints yet deliver well known lines or embody a familiar literary character in a completely original way is (I think) a worthy endeavor and, when it works, absolutely miraculous. David Tennant’s Hamlet springs to mind. Breathtaking in its originality and BECAUSE of its originality.
Gotta jump on some calls.
Winter.of.Discontext — 12/5/2023 2:44 PM
Digital photography
With acting, Christina definitely has a very original approach, and she avoids watching earlier productions if she’s working on a show.
I’d say the constraints do make the idea of originality a different thing when it comes to acting in particular. With production and script development and directing, one can really things in new directions if you want, and that all drives the acting. But if you’ve got a traditional production of a play that’s been around a while and the direction and so on are all done in line with past productions… that’s really gonna limit the originality that an actor can bring to a role. Not entirely, but to some extent
Debauch — 12/5/2023 3:34 PM
Interesting how people choose to approach it. By avoiding past productions one retains freedom and innocence to interpret lines and motivations in their own way, whereas watching past performances yields rewards as well. In my personal experience it depends on the show and type (dinner theater vs. musical theatre or interactive or Shakespeare). Like, how many past renditions of ‘Hello Dolly’ does one need to see?… And once done, to come to realize that the only thing that could possibly make the show palatable is if Tim Curry were to play Dolly and do it in drag. Winter.of.Discontext — 12/10/2023 12:34 AM
Well… it’s also extraordinarily unrealistic in the other direction. She’s almost impossibly voluptuous (leaving aside the fact that she seems to be part of her bike). This is an example of what I’d call the most common unrealistic beauty standard and, to me she just looks fake. Because of all that, and because of the painting like quality of the art. If that speaks to you, that’s great and I celebrate and support it. It’s just not my aesthetic. It looks like an overly idealized D&D illustration. Realistic, flawed, normal people or grossly unusual people that don’t fit in any standard beauty categories—sometimes also in very unsusual settings—is my wheelhouse on the whole, and I don’t consider my aesthetics to be standards that should be strived for, just complex representations of the human condition that I sometimes find beautiful and sometimes don’t. This is why I find I need to use real images, and less-produced images at that, to achieve a certain “real-like” effect. 

Which brings me to the other thing that makes me question others’ motives… which is that most of the source images that I use… well, of the ones that are photos of real people at all… are usually googled models that post their stuff on the internet. Sometimes fashion models, and sometimes small-time content creators. In the latter case, I consider them people who choose to empower themselves through the tools of the internet. That I find inspiring, especially as a sometimes content-creator myself. For both the fashion world and the content creator world, I tend to assume these are fairly empowering roles (compared to, say, burger flipper or Walmart cashier). Is this strictly true? Probably not. But both worlds tend to come with a certain degree of age verification at least, so I feel I’m on solid ground thinking that my images, being generated using of-age women, are not exploitative, or at least less exploitative than the fashion world (given that world is harvesting real girls for their images) 
My images, by contrast, are not of real people at all, and I feel they fall more into the category of Minnie Mouse or Harley Quinn than anything else. I just want them to have the illusion of being real-ish, less-idealized, or idealized in an alternative, jarring, or fantastical or alien way. 
One could argue that my work is a byproduct of the exploitation of the fashion industry, or of the excesses of online social media culture…. But to say it’s kiddie porn… I don’t buy it. That’s just someone projecting their personal ick factor, their personal beauty standards, or perhaps their discomfort at being turned on by something they don’t want to be turned on by. While I find some of the faces I create quite beautiful, most of them, even the nude ones, or the ones that project some kind of sexual trappings, I don’t find particularly sexually compelling. There are exceptions, of course, but for almost none of them do I find the sexual element to outweigh the complexity of the cross-cutting social commentary evident in the images.
Winter.of.Discontext — 12/10/2023 12:49 AM
The images are, mostly… um… I’m not sure how to put it… emotionally perplexing? They tend to put my… animal brain? Hormonally-driven feelings? In conflict with my social and ethical sensibilities and try to pick at the feelings that arise. Should I celebrate women for using their physical endowments to eke out some amount of power or income? Should I feel bad that they find their most marketable asset to be physical assets that are seen as lesser than their intellectual assets, even when it takes intellectual acumen to leverage and highlight one’s perceived physical beauty? Are there aspects of the images that seem to reflect some sense of interiority… that is, do these imaginary women have imaginary personalities that make them interesting, troubling, or otherwise compelling to think more deeply about?
Winter.of.Discontext — 12/10/2023 12:57 AM
That’s usually what I’m going for, although I definitely don’t achieve it all, or maybe not even  most of the time… It’s trial and error and curiosity to discover the surprises that invariably arise in the process. I try something, see if it looks like there’s something interesting to explore, then explore it or move on to the next image. I only revisit a small fraction of the content I create, because most isn’t that compelling. 
They’re byproducts of a process of discovery. And, as much as all of that are integral aspects of what I do, at least as often as not… it’s not the girls I’m trying to generate at all, they’re just the vehicle for trying out various prompt elements and style settings. I just need something as a base, and if it’s going to be a person, I’d usually rather look at women than men.
